**A length bug in M10.0 that this uncovered**, and it is not FPU-specific: for opclass 100/101 `shape()` left `fpw` at zero, so an IMMEDIATE operand was sized by the integer rule -- one longword -- when the instruction carries one per selected register.  `FMOVEM.L #x,FPCR/FPSR` was therefore two words short and everything after it decoded from the middle of the immediate.  That is wrong with or without an FPU (the LC040's format $4 frame stacks the NEXT instruction's PC), so the fix is not gated on `HAS_FPU`.  Catching it needed a deliberate test choice: with the second immediate longword ZERO the mis-parse decodes as `ORI.B #0,D0` and lands back on the right boundary, so the mutant survived -- `fpureal_cr.s` uses $0208 for it, which becomes `ORI.B #8,D0` and shows up in D0.

**The legality rules are WinUAE's and the reference's, and they are now enforced** (PLAN.md D20): Dn carries one register only, An only FPIAR, an immediate is a source only, a PC-relative destination is rejected, and `(An)+` and `-(An)` are allowed in BOTH directions -- which is where the PRM's prose and its own tables disagree and the hardware-validated source decides.  An illegal one takes the ordinary F-line, **format $0 with the instruction's own PC, not M10.0's format $4**, because with an FPU the effective address has been looked at; with `HAS_FPU = 0` nothing has, and WinUAE's LC040 path gives every well-formed cpid-1 opcode the format $4 frame, so that stays.

**The test is `tb/pipe_asm/fpureal_cr.s` + `.exp`, 28 checks**, and the case worth naming is 1: FPCR is not merely written and read back, it is PROVED to have reached the unit, because the same `1.0 / 3.0` divide is `...AAAA` truncated under round-to-zero and `...AAAB` under round-to-nearest.  A register that reads back but is not wired to the arithmetic would pass a read-back test and fail this one.  Then FPSR's INEX2 and accrued INEX from that divide; an immediate list of two; three registers from memory through `(A0)+` and back out through `(A5)+`; `-(An)`; the An form; the empty list; FPIAR surviving two control moves after an FADD at a known address; and the three illegal encodings, each checked as a DELTA against vector 11 / format $0 / own PC so the expect file holds zeros.

**Mutants for M10.2, all caught** (`fpr:fpureal_cr`):

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| C1 | `fp_cr_sel = 2'd0` | the register selection | everything lands in FPIAR: FPCR reads 0, the rounding never changes |
| C2 | `cr_nth`'s FPCR arm returns FPSR | the FPCR/FPSR/FPIAR order | the three-register round trip comes out permuted |
| C3 | `fp_bg <= !(fp_dn \|\| fp_done)` (the deadlock above) | the "no command was sent" case | the program hangs at the second instruction |
| C4 | `s.fpw = 4'd0` for opclass 100/101 | the immediate's length | D0 = $18: the stream decoded the second longword as `ORI.B #8,D0` |
| C5 | `d.imm[4:0] = 5'd4` | the per-register step | A0 walks by 4 instead of 12 and the round trip reads one register |
| C6 | the illegal-EA arm takes format $4 | D20's frame rule | the handler's resume is wrong and the program runs off |
| C7 | an immediate operand read as a register | the instruction-stream path | FPCR takes whatever was in the register |

**M10.3 -- step (d), the enabled arithmetic exceptions, BUILT 2026-09-23.**  Vectors 48-54 in both of their shapes, five mutants caught and one recorded as surviving.  It was not optional: M10.2 made FPCR writable, and the unit's enabled-exception arm (`ap040_fpu.v:1891`) does NOT pulse `done`, so without this the first program to enable an exception would hang EA-fetch -- which mutant E1 now demonstrates rather than asserts.

**Two shapes, and which one fires is decided by the release, not by the exception.**  Both are the reference's:
- the operation is **still in the pipeline** when the unit raises it -> POST-instruction, format $0, the NEXT instruction's PC (`ap040_core.v:4217`);
- it has **already been released** -> it cannot be reported against an instruction that has left, so it becomes PENDING and is taken in front of the next floating-point instruction, PRE-instruction, format $0, with THAT instruction's own PC (`:1944` and `:3600`).  FPIAR still names the one that faulted, which is what an FPSP handler reads -- and FPIAR is right because M10.1 step 3 wired it.

**Where it is raised, and why not where the design first said.**  Both come out of `fp_st()` inside `P_FPU`, not from a new term in `stepf`'s dispatch chain: that chain is the core's longest path and build `m9s` measured about 0.9 ns for one term in it.  The pending one is delivered by letting the instruction enter `P_FPU` as usual and raising it there before anything of the instruction starts -- no command, no operand beats -- so nothing new touches the chain at all.

**Three details that are easy to get wrong and are now pinned by mutants.**
1. `fp_exc_req` is **latched** into `fp_ae` exactly the way `fp_acc` and `fp_dn` are, because the unit can raise it in the same clock as `accepted`; unlatched, the release would win that clock and the exception would be reported against the wrong instruction (the reference's S_FPU_GO checks `exc_req` before `accepted` for the same reason).
2. **`fp_bg` must be cleared** when a released operation's exception becomes pending.  Forget it and the next FP instruction waits at `P_START` for ever -- the same hang M10.2 found for a different cause.  Mutant E2 is that, and it hangs.
3. `fp_pend` is cleared when the exception is taken (E6: leave it set and the same instruction traps for ever -- 2,664 traps before the bench gave up).

**What is NOT built, and it is a frame rule and not a hang.**  A STORE (opclass 011) with an enabled exception is the reference's other three arms -- format $3, the operand's EA, and the destination written first or not depending on the class (`:4239`-`:4300`).  The post-instruction arm here is gated on `!mem_dst && !st_dst` exactly as the reference gates it on `!fp_st`, so a store's enabled exception is **dropped, not mis-framed**, and it cannot hang: the unit pulses `done` on that path (`ap040_fpu.v:2156`).  Recorded here as the remaining gap of M10.3.

**The test is `tb/pipe_asm/fpureal_exc.s` + `.exp`, 15 checks**, and it is built so the two shapes are told apart rather than assumed.  Case 1 (DZ enabled, `1.0 / 0.0`) is laid out so the next instruction after the divide IS the next floating-point instruction, so both shapes predict the same stacked PC and the case checks the vector and the frame without depending on the unit's internal timing.  Case 2 (INEX2 enabled on a divide that IS released) puts integer work in between, so the two shapes predict DIFFERENT PCs -- and the measured one is the LATER floating-point instruction ($482), which is the pre-instruction delivery.  Case 3 clears the enables and runs the same operations clean, and the two registers the traps suppressed are stored: FP2 = 2.0 proves the post-instruction trap's RTE ran its instruction exactly once, FP3 = 7.0 the same for the pre-instruction one.  `vectors.inc` gained `v_fparith` for vectors 48-54, defaulting to `unexp` like the rest.

**Mutants for M10.3** (`fpr:fpureal_exc`):

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| E1 | `fp_ae` never latched | this instruction's own exception | **the core hangs** at the divide -- the unit raised the exception and never pulsed `done`, which is the failure mode this step exists to prevent |
| E2 | the released-operation arm disabled | the pending exception AND the `fp_bg` clear | one trap instead of two, then a hang at the next FP instruction |
| E3 | the pending one stacks `npc` | the pre-instruction rule | the stacked-PC delta is 8, and FP3 comes out $7FFF0000 (the instruction it was taken in front of never ran) |
| E4 | the post-instruction one stacks format $2 | the frame rule | the format/vector word reads $20C8 |
| E6 | `fp_pend` never cleared | the one-shot | 2,664 traps and no progress |
| -- | the same-clock race term of the pend condition | the exception that arrives in the very clock of the release | **SURVIVES, and that is understood**: this unit raises `exc_req` many clocks after `accepted`, so the coincidence does not occur with it.  The term stays -- it costs nothing and it makes the rule true rather than accidental -- and it is written down here rather than deleted |

**Step (d), the design as it was written before it was built** (kept as the record of what was decided and why):

**M10.4 -- FMOVEM of the floating-point registers (opclass 110/111) BUILT 2026-09-23.**  Twelve bytes per register, up to eight registers, both directions, with the 68040's two mask conventions.  Seven mutants, all caught.  This is the form compilers actually emit -- `FMOVEM.X FP2-FP7,-(SP)` on entry and `FMOVEM.X (SP)+,FP2-FP7` on exit -- so it is what makes an `HAS_FPU = 1` image able to run compiled floating-point code at all.

**The sequencer is the same three states again**, with a register list consumed as it goes: `FS_RD` reads three longwords into `fp_buf` and then writes the register through the unit's RAW port (`fm_*`: architecturally exact bits, no condition codes, no rounding), `FS_WR` reads that port and emits one beat per longword.  `fp_addr` walks up by four per beat from the effective address, which for `-(An)` is already `An - 12n` because EA-calc's explicit byte count does the subtraction -- so the descending hardware layout and the ascending walk produce the same memory image, which is what the manual describes.

**What the manual fixes and what it does not.**  M68000PM/AD FMOVEM (data registers), p. 5-85/5-86, is explicit for the three standard forms: a control mode transfers upward in the order FP0-FP7; predecrement is register-to-memory ONLY, decrements by 12 before each store, order FP7-FP0, and leaves An pointing at the last image stored; postincrement is memory-to-register ONLY, upward, order FP0-FP7.  All three come out of this implementation unchanged.  What the manual does not describe is an encoding whose MASK CONVENTION disagrees with its addressing mode -- a control-mode store carrying a predecrement-convention mask -- and there the reference and WinUAE define two quirks (PLAN.md D21): the mask is read from the other end, and each register's three longwords are written in REVERSED order.  `fpureal_mvm.s` case 3 pins both.

**Three bugs on the way, each worth the note.**
1. The decode cleared the source effective address for opclass 110 as well as 111.  A load's EA IS its source, so the address was never calculated and the instruction walked off into an address error.  The rule is now "the EA is the source for anything moving INTO the unit" -- opclass 010, 100 and 110.
2. The twelve-byte step was written `{3'd0,n} + {n,3'd0}`, which is 9n, not 12n.  With three registers that is 27 instead of 36, and the whole block landed on an odd address; the test showed it immediately as a misaligned image.
3. The write to the unit's raw port is armed one clock before it goes out (as the control-register write is), and the register selector had already moved on by then, so every register was written to FP0.  `fp_mwsel` captures it with the data.  The same hazard, the same shape, the second time -- worth remembering for FSAVE.

**Mutants for M10.4** (`fpr:fpureal_mvm`):

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| V1 | the step is 4n, not 12n | the per-register stride | the `-(An)` block lands 24 bytes off and the image is NOPs |
| V2 | `fp_rev = 0` | the reversed longwords | case 3's first longword is the exponent, not the low mantissa |
| V3 | `fp_lsb = 0` | the LSB-first walk of a predecrement store | `FP2` lands where `FP0` belongs |
| V4 | `fp_mvr` always `7 - b` | the predecrement mask convention | case 3 writes registers that were never loaded |
| V5 | `fp_fm_sel = fp_fsel` (no capture) | the armed write's selector | every register is written to FP0: the restore gives zeros |
| V6 | the load's `-(An)` rejection | one EA rule | two F-lines instead of three, and A1 left at `$51DC` |
| V7 | the store's `(An)+` and PC-relative rejections | the other two | one F-line instead of three, and A1 walked up by 36 |

**THE FPU MEASUREMENT BUILD, 2026-09-23: `AP040_HAS_FPU = 1` ROUTES CLEAN, AND THE WHOLE DESIGN FITS -- WITH 1,088 LUTs TO SPARE.**  `build/stage_ap040_pipe_m10fpu` in the integration worktree (sources `bbd7a56`, `AP040_PIPE_MMU=1 AP040_PIPE_FPU=1`, ILA off, `IMPL_EFFORT=high`, md5 `a5200ba6be701ce7f9269c646b59fbf1`).  **It is a MEASUREMENT build, not a gate image**: `stage_ap040_pipe_m9se` remains the only image Paul loads, and every gate and board image stays `HAS_FPU = 0` until he asks.

| | `m104` (HAS_FPU = 0, 61b6ed2) | **`m10fpu` (HAS_FPU = 1, bbd7a56)** |
|---|---|---|
| Slice LUTs | 31,638 (49.9 %) | **42,912 (67.7 %)** -- **+11,274** |
| WNS / TNS | -0.483 / -5.924 | **-0.483 / -6.042** |
| failing endpoints | 16, all the SDRAM path | **16, all the SDRAM path** (`dr_d[n] -> sdram/sdata_reg_reg[n]/D`, one per data bit, the family every reference build has).  **No CPU endpoint.** |
| clk_38 -> clk_114 | +0.296 | **+0.734** |
| clk_114 -> clk_38 | +0.015 | **+0.009** (m101 was +0.005: the same family, no regression) |
| clk_38 / clk_114 / clk_148 intra | +0.433 / +0.522 / +0.039 (m101) | **+0.100 / +0.114 / +0.022** |

**Three things this says.**  (1) **The stop rule passes**: one high-effort routed build, and its only violated endpoints are the sixteen SDRAM ones that every reference build has.  (2) **The FPU fits, but the margin is 1,088 LUTs against the 44,000 budget, not the ~5,500 the OOC number projected** -- the wrapper-OOC estimate of +6,697 became **+11,274** in the routed design, because the FPU's glue, the wider operand paths and the extra decode do not exist in isolation.  Q4's answer stands ("it fits") but its margin does not: anything else added to an FPU image has to come out of 1,088 LUTs, and the ILA (which costs ~6,300) does NOT fit alongside it.  (3) The intra-clock margins all FELL while the crossings rose, which is what a 68 %-full die looks like; nothing there is a CPU path.

**M9.T step 4 -- the nested-exception ordering, 2026-09-23, and it turned out to be two findings rather than one build.**

**(a) The interrupt recognised WHILE another exception is being processed (test 94) ALREADY WORKED.  What was missing was the BENCH.**  `tb_ap040_pipe_compat.v` ties `shim_state` to zero -- it is a leftover probe of the reference core's microcoded state machine -- so the `$F144` cycle-fine injector, which raises IPL2 when that state machine reaches state 34 (stacking) or 42 (after the vector read), never fired at all.  The bench nevertheless advertised the capability in `IPLCAP` ($F160 = $0007), so `t_exceptions.s` ran the block and failed test 94 without ever having had an interrupt injected.  The two points are named on THIS pipeline now -- `exc_stacking` = `P_EXC` with `x_step 0`, `exc_vecdone` = `P_EXC` with `x_step 4` -- and with them **tests 93, 94, 95, 96 and 102-105 pass**: the core takes the interrupt in front of the original handler's first instruction, which is what the M9 subset's design already did.  **No RTL change was needed for the coordinating session's item; the milestone's cost was in finding that out.**

**(b) A trace and an interrupt at the same boundary: three sources, three answers, and the MANUAL is the one to follow.**  This is D23, and it is the only place in this plan where the reference and WinUAE disagree with the manual AND with each other:
- **lib/AP68040**: interrupt first, the displaced trace delivered at the interrupt handler's entry (`texc`).  Its own comment calls this OPEN -- "it may be a spurious trace no real 68040 produces".
- **WinUAE**: interrupt first, trace DROPPED (`exception_check_trace` clears both T bits and the armed `DOTRACE`, and its `< 68040` guard means the 040 never re-arms it).
- **M68040UM 8.3, Table 8-4**: **Trace is priority group 6, Interrupt is group 8**, "with 0 as the highest priority" -- the TRACE goes first.  And 8.3 says what becomes of the loser: "As soon as the M68040 has completed exception processing for a condition when an interrupt exception is pending, it begins exception processing for the interrupt exception instead of executing the exception handler for the original exception condition", with the worked TRAP example ending "it is in the interrupt handler, which returns to the trap exception handler".

Authority rule 1 settles it: **the trace frame is built first, and the still-pending interrupt is then taken in front of the TRACE HANDLER's first instruction.**  The change is one line -- the trace override is applied outside the interrupt's instead of being suppressed by it -- because the "interrupt in front of the handler's first instruction" behaviour was already there.  The interrupt is not acknowledged by the trace's entry (`tr_st` clears the `irq` flag), so the request simply stands.

**`t_exceptions.s` tests 142/143 therefore cannot pass on this core and are not meant to**: they assert the reference's model.  `tb/irq_asm/t_trirq_pipe.s` asserts the manual's instead, and it does it DETERMINISTICALLY rather than by sweeping a delay: a level-2 request is raised under mask 7, and the instruction that lowers the mask is itself traced, so both exceptions are pending at that boundary every time.  Two legs, T1 and T0.

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| W1 | `!irq_go` back in `tr_go` -- the behaviour before this step | the manual's priority | the trace is lost: the interrupt frame's stacked PC is the traced instruction's successor, not the trace handler |
| W2 | the `&& !irq_go` exclusion in the yield term | the priority for a T0 trace | the T0 leg loses its trace the same way |
| W3 | the trace's entry claims the interrupt's acknowledge (`t.irq = 1`) | "the request simply stands" | the interrupt is acknowledged without being taken and never arrives |

**What is left of M9, sized rather than absorbed.**  Two items, and they are NOT the same size:
1. **`t_exceptions.s` tests 136/137 and the withdrawal sweeps -- a CALIBRATION item, perhaps half a day.**  Test 136 sweeps an IPL arrival delay of 1..20 clocks across a `MOVE to SR` that raises the mask and requires the sweep to STRADDLE it (some taken, some not).  It is not a semantic check -- the rule itself is enforced by the bench's `tb_must` IPEND shadow -- but the window is calibrated to the reference core's instruction timing, and this core is faster.  It needs the sweep range re-derived for this pipeline (and the bench's `tb_must` shadow, which is also tied to `shim_state`, given real signals) rather than any RTL work.
2. **The rest of `exc_m9t2` is blocked by something else entirely**: with the M-bit section in, the program's layout shifts and the fetch-bus-error test lands its armed address ($E5A) inside a longword fetch this core issues at $E58, so handler 13 rejects it in the two slower bus profiles.  That is a BENCH/program-layout coincidence, not a core defect -- `exc_m9t` without the M-bit section passes all three profiles -- but it means the M-bit section cannot simply be switched on in the suite.  Sizing: the honest fix is to teach the fetch-berr injector to match a longword-aligned fetch containing the armed address, half a day, and it belongs with item 1 as one "finish the t_exceptions extract" task.

**M11 closed, 2026-09-23: the routed build and the placement histogram.**

**The routed timing build** `build/stage_ap040_pipe_m11a2` (RTL as of `040879d`, the shipping configuration -- MMU on, **FPU OFF**, ILA off, `IMPL_EFFORT=high`, md5 `3f5f7956f32415dc1ba043af754bce45`):

| | m104 (61b6ed2) | **m11a2 (040879d)** |
|---|---|---|
| Slice LUTs | 31,638 (49.9 %) | **32,113 (50.65 %)**, +475 for the trace, M-bit and nested-exception work |
| WNS / TNS | -0.483 / -5.924 | **-0.349 / -5.316** (better) |
| failing endpoints | 16, all SDRAM | **16, all SDRAM** (`dr_d[n] -> sdata_reg_reg[n]`), **no CPU endpoint** |
| clk_38 -> clk_114 | +0.296 | **+0.709** |
| **clk_114 -> clk_38** | +0.015 | **+0.000** |
| intra clk_38 / clk_114 / clk_148 | -- | +0.124 / +0.634 / +0.203 |

It PASSES the stop rule: one high-effort routed build, and the only violated endpoints are the sixteen the SDRAM pins have in every reference build.  **The one number to keep naming is `clk_114 -> clk_38` at +0.000** -- the same family as +0.005 (m101), +0.015 (m104) and +0.009 (m10fpu), and now exactly at the limit.  It MEETS; by Q18's refinement the signal would be two consecutive FAILURES on it, which this is not.  But four builds in a row under 0.02 ns, one of them at zero, is the M11.1 acknowledge-to-IF family saying it has no margin left to give, and the next change that touches EA-fetch's redirect should expect to pay for it.

**The `sim/ddr3_cpu` placement histogram**, the other M11 exit item, run for the first time with the PIPELINED core (`PIPELINED=1 RUNTAG=m11place REALSDRAM=1 ./run.sh --ap040` in the integration worktree, which already had the leg): **PASS -- 2 passed, 0 failed, and the independent backdoor read of the DDR3 array matches the pattern.**  The placement line is the one that matters:

    placement: 6447 chip-RAM acknowledges (6383 reads, 64 writes, 0 off 2/6/10/14/13)

**Every chip-RAM WRITE acknowledge is on the board grid**, which is the property the whole D3/E1 placement machinery exists to protect (a write landing off-grid is what corrupted chip RAM).  The reads are spread across all sixteen phases (361-446 each) rather than clustering, which is a difference from the reference core's read mix recorded in the bench-traps note (3/7/11/15) -- and bench-trap 6 says exactly this: judge reads and writes SEPARATELY, because the combined peak moves with the workload and with the core's own request timing.  The write grid is the judged check and it is clean.

**The cputest corpus beyond Basic, 2026-09-23 -- six groups replayed, ZERO failing rounds, and a stale skip filter that is the real finding.**

| group | slices | rounds run | failing | skipped as unsupported | note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **AE** (address error: Bcc/BSR/JMP/JSR/RTE/RTR/RTS to odd targets) | **38/38** | **357,106** | **0** | 1,071,318 | the M6 format $2 frames, at scale |
| **ODD_STK** | **6/6** | 3,832 | **0** | 11,496 | -- |
| **FFEXT_DST** | **756/756** | 30,272 | **0** | 0 | -- |
| **FFEXT_SRC** | **4,632/4,632** | 30,126 | **0** | 0 | -- |
| **IRQ** | 68/117 | 18,352 | **0** | 275,529 | the other 49 slices ran ZERO rounds |
| **ODD_IRQ** | 0/8 | 0 | -- | 1,024 | every round skipped: the group is vacuous today |

**439,688 rounds run across the six groups and not one failing round.**  That is the M6 access-error work and the M9 interrupt work meeting a corpus generated from a UAE core validated against real silicon, and it agrees with them.

**But the skip filter is stale, and it is the same class of bug as the dead bench probes.**  `tests/cputest/replay/tb_dat_replay_pipe.v` skips a round "because the core has no such feature yet" when it sees:
- **trace**: T1/T0 set in the input SR, a trace record, or vector 9 -- *"about 3/4 of every slice, because the generator runs each test under S/T0/T1/M variants"*;
- **interrupts**: an IPL level set, or vectors 24-31;
- bus error (vector 2), odd-vector groups, maintenance records.

**The core has had interrupts since the M9 subset and trace since M9.T steps 1-2.**  So three quarters of every slice in the corpus -- including Basic's 3.2 million rounds -- is being thrown away on a claim that stopped being true two milestones ago, ODD_IRQ is entirely vacuous, and 49 of IRQ's 117 slices report "failing" only because ZERO rounds ran (the runner counts a slice with no rounds as failed, which is the right default and is exactly how this was noticed).

**Sizing it, rather than absorbing it**: enabling the TRACE rounds is the cheap half and the bigger win -- the T bits come from the record's own SR, no external stimulus is needed, and the frame checks the bench already makes for other vectors apply unchanged; **half a day to a day**, and it would quadruple the corpus coverage of every group.  The INTERRUPT rounds are harder because the record encodes a request the bench must drive at the right moment against this core's own sampling rules; **a day or two**, and D23 says trace-and-interrupt coincidences will legitimately differ from WinUAE there, so those records need the divergence applied rather than asserted.  Neither is started; both are recorded here because "three quarters of the oracle is switched off" is worth more as a written finding than as a quiet fix.  **UPDATE 2026-09-24: the INTERRUPT half is DONE** (and the odd-vector groups with it): IRQ 117/117 slices, **293,881 rounds, 0 failing** (was 18,352 run, 275,529 skipped); ODD_IRQ 8/8, 256 rounds (was 0); ODD_EXC 33/33, 163,980 rounds (was skipped); AE/FFEXT_SRC/FFEXT_DST re-run unchanged.  How, and the one bench pitfall it hit (pins asserted a clock late let D17 legitimately defer the interrupt past 167 MOVE-to-CCR rounds), in `tests/cputest/replay/README.md` and the log row.  The TRACE half is still skipped.

**M11, the measurement pass, 2026-09-23 -- all four numbers, and one recommendation.**

**(3) Retire rate against the reference, on the workload the plan names.**  `dhry` does NOT exist in this tree: `lib/AP68040/tb/build_tests.sh` has no Dhrystone target and none is built, so the plan's "dhry" is a name without a file and is recorded as such.  `bench_loop.s` does exist and is the closest stand-in -- a loop-heavy, locality-rich program written as a MEASUREMENT program for exactly this kind of question -- and it runs on each core's own program bench with the same memory model and caches on:

| | reference | pipelined |
|---|---|---|
| `bench_loop.s`, phase 0 | 325,348 cycles | **261,638 cycles** |
| ratio | | **0.804x** |

Same program, same instruction count, so the cycles ratio IS the retire-rate ratio: **1.24x more instructions per clock**.  **Two UNRELATED workloads landing on 0.804x and 0.799x is the strongest throughput evidence in this plan**: `bench_loop` is a synthetic, cache-friendly inner loop measured on each core's own program bench, and Kickstart 3.1.4's boot is 2.3 million instructions of real operating-system code -- ROM and RAM, exceptions, device registers, DMA -- measured on a different bench entirely.  A single benchmark can flatter a microarchitecture; two of that shape agreeing to half a percent is a property of the core, not of the test.

**(4) The port-conflict profile, which is the M14 gate -- stated as a number with its criterion.**  In this core IF is the LOWEST-priority user of the shared memory port (`ap040_pipe_bcu.v`: split byte > store > data read > instruction fetch), so the question is how often IF wants the port while a DATA access holds it.  The counter is now in the compat bench (`PORTCONF`, replacing the reference-shaped `memlat_portwait` that was tied off), and it excludes cycles where the port is busy with IF's own fetch.

| workload | cycles | IF denied | share of ALL cycles | share of IF's own requests |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `bench_loop` (loop-heavy, the dhry stand-in) | 261,638 | 51,634 | **19 %** | 49 % |
| `texc_m9t` (exception-heavy) | 36,576 | 5,741 | **15 %** | 64 % |
| `t_cache` | 2,190 | 278 | 12 % | 62 % |
| `t_integer` (straight-line integer) | 11,210 | 832 | 7 % | 40 % |

**The plan's criterion is "> 15 % of cycles justifies M14".  On the loop-heavy workload it is 19 %, on the exception-heavy one exactly 15 %, and on straight-line integer code 7 %.**  So the gate is MET by its closest available stand-in, and M14 is justified on throughput grounds -- **but it is not affordable in the configuration that matters**: the routed FPU build leaves 1,088 LUTs of the 44,000 budget, and split I/D caches cost far more than that.  **M14 is therefore FILED WITH PAUL'S CHOICE OF IMAGE, not left as a milestone waiting its turn**: *justified on throughput for an LC040 image, unaffordable in an FPU image at 1,088 LUTs spare*.  It becomes schedulable the moment he says which core he wants, and it is not schedulable before that.  (Section 9 carries the same statement where he will read it.)

**(5) The branch guess, bounded before anything is built.**  ID guesses every `Bcc` TAKEN.  Counting the Kickstart boot trace against the ROM (`bcc_count.py`, 2,535,156 retired instructions, 90 % of their PCs decodable -- the rest run from RAM):

| | count | share of retired |
|---|---|---|
| `Bcc` taken | 141,775 | 5.6 % |
| **`Bcc` NOT taken** | **101,390** | **4.00 %** |
| ... of them the BYTE form | 99,040 | 97.7 % of the not-taken |
| ... the WORD form | 2,350 | 2.3 % |

And the penalty is not uniform: the cycle table measures `Bcc.W` not taken at 5 against a manual 3 and `Bcc.L` at 6 against 3, but the BYTE form -- 97.7 % of the not-taken branches -- measures **`bne.s` + `MOVEQ` retiring in 2 clocks for the pair**, i.e. at or below the manual, because its fall-through is already in the prefetch buffer.  So:

- **realistic bound**: 2,350 word-form not-taken branches x 2 clocks = ~4,700 cycles of 26,758,312 = **0.018 %**;
- **pessimistic bound**, pretending every not-taken `Bcc` paid the two clocks the word form pays: 101,390 x 2 = 202,780 = **0.76 %** (0.84 % if the 10 % of RAM-resident code branches at the same rate).

**Both are under the 2 % line, so this is MEASURED AND DECLINED**: a perfect branch predictor could recover at most three quarters of one percent of the boot, and a backward-taken/forward-not-taken heuristic -- the cheap option, no storage -- would recover only part of that while losing on backward not-taken branches.  Neither is worth a new structure to verify.  **The two OVER rows in the cycle table stay, with this as their reason: they are real, they are attributable, and they do not matter at the system level.**

**M11 first numbers, 2026-09-23 (both of the ones the plan asks for).**

**(1) Per-instruction, against Section 10** (`tests/cycles/run_cycles.py`, 145 forms, zero-wait memory, median retire-to-retire over 64 repetitions, rule `measured <= manual + 1`): **143 of 145 within the rule, and exactly TWO sit more than one clock above the manual.**

| form | measured | manual (10-5) | over by | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `Bcc.W` NOT taken | 5 | 3 | +2 | ID GUESSES every Bcc taken, so a not-taken branch pays a redirect correction.  EA-fetch resolves it and corrects there -- "a two-clock correction instead of the four a redirect from EX costs" -- and two clocks is exactly what shows up here |
| `Bcc.L` NOT taken | 6 | 3 | +3 | the same, plus the longer displacement's extra fetch word |

**Do not "fix" those two rows.**  The measurement pass below counted the Kickstart boot: **97.7 % of all not-taken `Bcc` are the BYTE form, and the byte form measures at or BELOW the manual** (`bne.s` + `MOVEQ` retire in two clocks for the pair, because the fall-through is already in the prefetch buffer).  Only the word and long forms pay the correction, and they are 2.3 % of the not-taken branches, which is why the system cost of the guess is 0.018 % of the boot.  These rows are real and attributable; they are not worth a predictor, and a future agent who removes the guess to make them green will lose the taken case (94 % of `Bcc` that ARE taken retire in 2 clocks precisely because of it).

Everything else is at or below the manual, including the forms that matter most for the system number: `Bcc` taken 2 (manual 2), `DBcc` false 2 (manual 3), `MOVEM.L` of four registers 6 (manual 6), `MOVE16` 8 (8), the bitfield forms, `CAS.L` 1 against a manual 37, and the whole ALU/EA matrix at 1-4.  **The branch guess is therefore the core's one measurable throughput defect against the manual**, and it is a DESIGN choice with a known alternative (a real predictor, or guessing backward-taken / forward-not-taken).  Nothing is changed on the strength of this table alone: the system number below says what it is worth.

**(2) The system number: Kickstart 3.1.4 to trackdisk's first track read, both cores, same ROM, same bench.**

| | reference (lib/AP68040) | pipelined (`5494007`, LC040) |
|---|---|---|
| instructions | 2,289,114 | 2,288,738 |
| **cycles** | **33,496,750** | **26,758,312** |
| cycles per instruction | 14.633 | **11.691** |

**0.799x the reference's cycles -- the 0.80x figure is held, and very slightly beaten.**  Put the other way round, the pipelined core is **1.25x faster** on the same work.  The margin is thin enough to be worth watching: 0.80 is the figure to HOLD, so any later change that costs more than ~0.3 % of cycles moves it the wrong side of the target, and this table is the one to re-run.

**The `t_exceptions.s` oracle table (M9.T), kept the way the `t_fpu.s` one is -- including the rows that FAIL ON PURPOSE.**

| section / test | what it exercises | result | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| TRAP, illegal/BKPT, A/F-line, CHK, divide by zero, TRAPV/TRAPcc, MOVEC matrix, MOVE USP, MOVES, user-mode round trip, format error, address error, physical bus error, the immediate group | the M9-subset sections | **PASS** (in the suite as `compat:texc_m9t`) | -- |
| trace (57, 58, 97, 98, 59, 99, 100) | T1, T0, the synchronisation list, the traced STOP, the MOVEC direction rule | **PASS** | M9.T steps 1-2 |
| interrupts, the level-sensitive IPL section | levels, masks, holds, withdrawal, the NetBSD shape | **PASS** | M9 subset |
| M bit throwaway (42, 43, 44) | the format $1 frame and the master/interrupt stack switch | **PASS** when the section is extracted (`exc_m9t2`) | M9.T step 3; `t_mbit_pipe.s` is the suite's regression test |
| 93-96, 102-105 | an interrupt recognised WHILE another exception is being processed | **PASS** | M9.T step 4 -- and they only ever ran once the bench's injector was wired |
| 90, 91 | the RTE that unmasks a pending level takes it at its own boundary | **PASS** | M9 subset |
| 136, 137 | the IPEND claim swept across a mask raise | **not run: the sweep window is the REFERENCE core's timing** | replaced by `t_ipend_pipe.s`, which sweeps 1..40 and withdraws a non-qualifying arrival.  The rule itself is the bench's now-live `tb_must` shadow |
| **142, 143** | a trace and an interrupt at the same boundary | **DELIBERATELY FAILING** | **They assert lib/AP68040's model -- interrupt first, the displaced trace delivered at the INTERRUPT handler's entry.  This core follows M68040UM 8.3 instead (trace first, priority group 6 against 8), which is D23.  `t_trirq_pipe.s` asserts the manual's ordering deterministically.  This row is kept rather than removed: a deliberately-failing test with a reason is evidence, a missing one is a hole** |
| the fetch-berr block in the M-bit section | a physical bus error on an instruction fetch | **blocked** | the FA the frame reports for a longword fetch, `$E58` against the armed `$E5A` -- an open question, see step 5 |

**M9.T step 5 -- the bench work step 4 uncovered, DONE 2026-09-23, and the sweep for the same class.**  The finding of step 4 was that `tb_ap040_pipe_compat.v` had been ported by tying the reference core's internal probes to zero, and that three interrupt ASSERTIONS and the IPEND shadow went on compiling while watching nothing.  They are live now, on this pipeline's own signals, and two of them had to be RESTATED rather than rewired -- which is the real lesson: an assertion written against a microcoded core is not automatically true of a pipelined one.

| assertion | was | is now |
|---|---|---|
| the IPEND shadow (`tb_must`): a qualified request must be taken at a following boundary | `shim_state == 4/34` edges, all tied to zero | the pipeline's own events: `insn_start` = EA-fetch's `st.fin`, `exc_accept` = the entry into `P_EXC`.  **The tolerance is CALIBRATED, not copied**: the reference allowed ONE instruction start, this core needs more because `irq_take` does not arm while a data read is outstanding (the M9 operand-read rule), and the measured worst case across the interrupt programs is **six**, in `t_irq_pipe`'s own read-to-clear case.  Sixteen is the bound, with the re-derivation recipe at the line |
| the phantom-interrupt and mask invariants | same dead probes | `exc_accept`, `x_lvl`, and the SR the bench already had |
| "any fetch during exception processing is the handler's opcode, FC=6" | true of a core that stops fetching while it stacks | **restated**: here IF keeps prefetching the INTERRUPTED stream until the entry's redirect, and FC=2 is correct for it if that stream was user code.  The check now follows the vector read's target and asserts the FC of the fetch AT the handler's entry address |
| "any data cycle during exception processing is FC=5" | same | **restated and moved off the bus**: a posted store from the interrupted instruction can still be draining, correctly as FC=1.  The rule is asserted where the exception's OWN accesses are issued -- its frame stores (`exe_o.exc && st_v`) and its vector read |
| the arming window "outside states 34..42" | a state range | **restated as what it meant**: from entering `P_EXC` until the entry's SR write has actually reached SR, because in this core the phase ends when the final micro-op is DISPATCHED and its SR write lands one or two clocks later in WB.  Without this the shadow armed a phantom claim in that gap on every single interrupt |

**Still inert, and named here rather than left looking alive** (the sweep the coordinating session asked for):
- `shim_state` itself: there is no microcoded state machine to expose.  Every assertion that needed it now uses a named pipeline event; the wire remains only because the `+exctrace` plusarg and the queue invariant below still reference it.
- **the X2.2 queue invariant** (`exception_prefetch` must not run with a queue fetch outstanding, states 178/179): **no equivalent here** -- this pipeline has no exception-prefetch stage; IF's buffer is flushed by the redirect and re-armed by the handler's own fetch.  Left inert deliberately, with the reason at the line.
- `shim_epf_pend`, `shim_lk_cyc`: the same, tied off with nothing to watch.
- **`+exctrace`'s FP register dump prints zeros** -- the reference read its register file directly.  A vector-55 trace would therefore name the exception but not the operand.  Cheap to fix when someone needs it (the FPU's registers are reachable through the tie include); recorded, not fixed.
- **`IPLCAP` ($F160 = $0007) advertises what the bench can do**, and step 4 proved it had been lying about bit 1 for the whole port.  It is honest again now, and it is worth treating that word as something to CHECK when a bench is ported, not carried over.

**`t_exceptions.s` test 136 could not simply be turned on**, and this is the second half of the calibration lesson: its sweep aims IPL arrivals 1..20 clocks at a mask-raising MOVE to SR and requires the sweep to STRADDLE the raise.  That window is a property of the reference core's instruction timing, and this core is faster, so the range does not straddle here -- and the program is the reference's, which this plan does not edit.  `tb/irq_asm/t_ipend_pipe.s` is the pipelined replacement: a wider sweep (1..40), the device WITHDRAWING an arrival that did not qualify so it cannot leak into the next pass, and a check that both sides were seen.  The rule itself is the bench's `tb_must` shadow, which is now live, so the program only has to prove the sweep exercised it.

**What is still open, with the evidence, rather than skipped**: the M-bit section of `t_exceptions.s` cannot join the suite extract because of the fetch-bus-error test.  With that section in, the program's layout shifts so that the armed FETCH berr address is `$E5A`, and the frame this core builds reports **FA = `$E58`** -- the LONGWORD-aligned address of the fetch that covered it -- where the reference reports `$E5A`.  Handler 13 rejects that in the two slower bus profiles.  **This is not obviously a bench bug**: M68040UM's format $7 FA field is "the address of the faulted access", and for a longword instruction fetch that is arguably the aligned address.  It needs a corpus record or a hardware capture to settle, so it is recorded here as an open question rather than papered over in the bench, and the M-bit section stays out of the suite until it is answered.

**M9.T step 3 -- the M bit, the master stack and the format $1 THROWAWAY frame, BUILT 2026-09-23.**  Seven mutants, all caught.  With it, `t_exceptions.s`'s own M-bit checks (42, 43, 44) pass.

**What the 68040 does** (M68040UM 8.2.9, 8.4.2), and it is not the order the summary sentence suggests: an INTERRUPT taken while S = 1 and M = 1 stacks TWO frames.  The NORMAL frame goes on the **master** stack -- the one the SR selects at the time -- and then the processor CLEARS M and stacks a four-word format $1 **throwaway** frame on the **interrupt** stack, which is where the handler runs.  The throwaway's SR image keeps M SET (only S is forced), and that is the whole trick: the RTE that pops it restores an SR with M set, so A7 becomes the master stack again and the RTE's format $1 continuation -- built back in M6 and tested by `rte_formats.s` -- finds the real frame there.  Only an INTERRUPT does this; a TRAP with M set stacks one frame on the master stack and leaves M alone.

**In this core** it is a second pass through `P_EXC`: at the end of the frame walk, an interrupt with M set re-arms the walk with format $1, the SR image OR'd with $2000, `x_bank` = ISP and one clock (`x_step 6`) to read the interrupt stack pointer, which `ra_a` hands over because `x_pass2` is set.  Two stack pointers move in the end, so the master's new value rides on the final micro-op's **u1**.

**The bug, and it is a port-map trap worth naming**: the first version put it on **u0**, and it vanished.  `ap040_execute.v` maps a sequenced `sp_v` onto u0 ("a sequenced stack-pointer write goes out on the u0 port"), so anything else placed there is silently overwritten -- no warning, no X, just a stack pointer that never moved.  The symptom was three instructions away: the RTE returned to `start` in user mode and the program died on a privilege violation whose stacked PC was $400.  **If a micro-op already writes a stack pointer, its spare address-register slot is u1.**

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| V1 | the throwaway pass never starts | the second frame | the handler stands on the master stack, check 1 |
| V2 | the SR image loses its M bit | the way back to the master stack | the RTE returns to `start` in user mode: a privilege violation |
| V3 | M is not cleared by the final micro-op | half the point of the frame | check 8: the master stack is not unwound |
| V4 | the master stack's new value (the u1 write) is dropped | exactly the bug above | the same privilege violation |
| V5 | the throwaway is written as format $0 | its format | check 8: the RTE pops eight bytes as a final frame and returns to the wrong place |
| V6 | pass 2 keeps reading the master stack | the stack switch | both frames land on the master stack |
| V7 | the `x_irq` qualifier dropped | "only an interrupt" | check 10: a TRAP with M set builds a throwaway too |

**The oracle, and where it now stops.**  `irq_asm/t_mbit_pipe.s` (ten checks) is the regression test and is in the suite's compat legs.  `t_exceptions.s`'s "M bit throwaway" section passes its own three checks and then stops at **test 94**, which needs a rule nothing here has built: an interrupt recognised WHILE another exception is being processed is stacked and vectored to BEFORE the original handler's first instruction (the reference's `S_EXC_JMP` `irq_pend` arm).  `mk_tint.py` carries the `exc_m9t2` extract ready for the day that lands; the suite still runs `exc_m9t`.

**What is left of M9 after this**, recorded so the next agent does not have to rediscover it: (a) that interrupt-during-exception rule, test 94; (b) the trace an interrupt DISPLACES at the same boundary -- the 68040 delivers it at the interrupt handler's first instruction (the reference's `texc` machinery), and this core drops it, which is `t_exceptions.s` tests 142/143; (c) the cycle-fine injector tests 93-105 and 136/137 that follow them.

**M9.T step 2 -- T0: the trace of CHANGES OF FLOW, BUILT 2026-09-23, and with it the REFERENCE'S OWN TRACE SECTION PASSES.**  Seven mutants caught, one recorded as surviving (and it found dead code).  `t_exceptions.s`'s trace section -- tests 57, 58, 97, 98, 59, 99 and 100, the T1 case, the T0 cases, the traced STOP and the MOVEC direction rule -- now runs on the compat leg and passes, so the suite's extract moved from `exc_m9s` to the new **`exc_m9t`**, which keeps every earlier section and adds `trace`.

**What T0 traces, and where each piece comes from.**  A taken branch or a return, and the instructions the 68040 counts as pipeline synchronisation points.  The second list is `t0_special`, **copied verbatim from the reference core into `ap040_decode.v`** -- comments included -- for the reason D22's FPSP opmode list is copied: two hand-written copies of a list this arbitrary disagree eventually, and the reference's was narrowed against hardware (its comments name the cputest runs that removed `MOVE USP,An` and the dynamic BSET encodings, and that fixed MOVEC to the WRITE direction only).  Decode carries it in one new `id_t` bit, `t0sync`, and sets the same bit for the FP forms the reference marks with `t0_force`: every FDBcc, FMOVEM of the control registers TO memory, and an FMOVEM register STORE.

**The conditional branch is the case only EA-fetch can answer**, and it is free there: ID GUESSES every Bcc/DBcc taken, so the NOT-taken one is the one that redirects, and `br_taken` -- which EA-fetch already computes for that correction -- is the answer either way.  No new port, no new stage crossing.

**Two rules that are not the obvious ones.**
1. **A traced STOP never enters the stopped state.**  Under T1 always; under T0 only when the SR it loads CHANGES T1/T0/S/M or the interrupt mask (the reference's changed-bits rule, from WinUAE's MakeFromSR returning before its trace decision).  `stepf` gives the traced STOP its `fin` and the trace is taken in front of the instruction after it.
2. **A T0 change-of-flow trace is resolved only once the TARGET has entered the pipeline**, so an ILLEGAL there WINS and cancels the trace (the reference's `flow_t0_pend`).  A T1 trace is not: it is taken at the boundary before the target is looked at.  One register, `tr_yield`, carries the difference.

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| U1 | conditional branches never count as flow | the taken Bcc | eight traces instead of nine |
| U2 | conditional branches ALWAYS count | the not-taken rule | ten traces: the NOT-taken beq traces too |
| U3 | `t0sync` dropped from the T0 event | the synchronisation list | four traces, and the first is the branch, not the NOP |
| U4 | `t0_special`'s MOVEC arm reads `$4E7A` instead of `$4E7B` | the direction rule hardware narrowed | **SURVIVED the first version of the test** -- see below -- and is caught by the frame sums |
| U5 | the STOP changed-bits test forced false | the traced STOP | the STOP stops for ever: five traces and no halt |
| U6 | the traced STOP does not get its `fin` | the other half of the same rule | it never leaves EA-fetch: the program stops at the STOP |
| U7 | `tr_yield` forced low | "the target's exception wins" | ten traces: the branch to the ILLEGAL traces as well |
| -- | `&& !tr_arm` added to the `ph <= P_STOP` assignment | nothing | **SURVIVES, and that is the finding**: `if (st.fin) ph <= P_START` at the end of the same block is the LATER assignment and wins, so the guard was dead code.  It was removed and the reason written at the line |

**The lesson of U4 is 2.1 item 6 in a new disguise.**  The first `trace_t0.exp` checked the COUNT, the first frame and the last frame -- and a mutation that makes the MOVEC READ trace instead of the MOVEC WRITE keeps all three: two neighbouring instructions simply swap roles, exactly the symmetric permutation a round trip cannot see.  The handler now also SUMS every frame's stacked PC and address field, and the mutant dies on both sums.  **A count plus the ends is not a check on the middle.**

**M9.T step 1 -- T1: the trace of EVERY instruction, BUILT 2026-09-23.**  Seven mutants, all caught.  M9's remainder is being built as its own milestone with its own accounting, in the order the coordinating session set: T1 and T0 on ordinary instructions first (what a debugger and NetBSD need), then the M bit, the master stack and the throwaway frame.  That it also unblocks `t_fpu.s` §18 is a side effect and is not counted as M10's.

**How it is built, and the one piece of luck worth writing down.**  A trace is a POST-instruction exception: the instruction completes and the trace is taken in front of the NEXT one, vector 9, format $2, with the stacked PC = the next instruction and the format-$2 address field = the PC of the instruction that was traced (the reference's `fetch_next` does exactly this, `exc(VEC_TRACE, 4'd2, pc, pc_i)`).  The T bits must be sampled at the START of an instruction -- the MOVE to SR that SETS T1 is not itself traced and the one that CLEARS it is -- and here that needs no register of its own: an instruction's own SR write commits at ITS writeback, which is after it has left EA-fetch, and `sr_in` is the write-through of that commit, so `sr_in` while the instruction is still in EA-fetch IS its starting SR.  Arming at `st.fin` reads exactly the value the rule asks for.  Nothing can move it underneath, because every SR writer redirects and so has no younger instruction in flight beside it.

**The bug, and the difference between an interrupt and a trace.**  The first build delivered ONE trace where the test expected seven, with the frame of the LAST traced instruction.  M9's interrupt is armed in a register and overrides the dispatch without blocking it, which is correct for an interrupt -- it may be taken at any later boundary -- but a trace belongs to exactly ONE boundary.  With the instruction after the traced one free to dispatch, its own `st.fin` simply overwrote the arm.  The fix keeps the timing shape that m9s and m9sb paid for: `tr_take` is a flip-flop that is ANDed into the read request and that forces `disp`/`issue` low at `P_START` only -- an AND with a register, not a term in `stepf`'s chain and not a mux the memory request is computed through.

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| T1 | `tr_take <= 1'b0` at the arm | the trace itself | no trace at all |
| T2 | the boundary hold (`hold && ph == P_START`) | the difference above | four traces instead of seven, and the first frame is a later instruction's |
| T3 | `t.ef = 4'd0` | the format $2 frame | the handler's format/vector check fails and its `8(sp)` reads the program, not an address field |
| T4 | `t.epc = tpc` | the stacked PC | the RTE returns to the traced instruction: 365 traces and the program never ends |
| T5 | `t.eaddr = npc` | the address field | it reports the next instruction instead of the traced one |
| T6 | the arm is not cleared by an exception | "the TRAP takes only the TRAP" | the handler's own first instruction traces for ever |
| T7 | `ph != P_EXC` dropped from the arm | exception entry is not a traced instruction | thirteen traces: the handler traces itself |

**Known gaps, deliberately left for the next steps and recorded here rather than discovered later**: T0 (change of flow) and its `t0_special` list, which is copied verbatim from the reference the way D22's FPSP list was; a traced STOP, which must not enter the stopped state; and what becomes of a trace an INTERRUPT displaces at the same boundary -- the 68040 delivers it at the interrupt handler's first instruction (the reference's `texc` machinery), and today's code drops it.  `t_exceptions.s`'s own trace section is the exit oracle and is turned on by a new `mk_tint.py` extract; it is not claimed until all three are in.

**M10.9 -- FMOVEM with a DYNAMIC register mask, BUILT 2026-09-23 (`0496985`, and the zero-step fix below `bbd7a56`), and with it `t_fpu.s` runs with NO SKIPS AT ALL.**  **Five** mutants, all caught; `fpureal_mvm.s` grew from 41 checks to 49.

The mask comes from the data register extension bits 6:4 name, so the register COUNT -- and with it the `(An)+` / `-(An)` step -- is not known until the instruction runs.  That is what M10.7's `an_ov` hook exists for and this is its second user: `reg_c` carries the register to EA-fetch, which reads it on `op_c`, computes twelve times the population count, walks the base back itself for a predecrement store, and hands An the new value through the override.

**The bug `t_fpu.s` found the moment the skip was removed, and it is a trap worth naming**: giving a dynamic list a step of ZERO does not mean "do not step".  `eacomp`'s contract is `nb == 0` -> use the SIZE rule, so a zero step silently became four, and a one-register `FMOVEM.X D0,-(A0)` moved A0 by four instead of twelve (`t_fpu.s` test 59 checks exactly that).  A dynamic list now decodes with ONE register's worth, 12, and EA-fetch adds the remaining 12 x (n-1).

**The test case is written the way M10.7's lesson says**: a dynamic load followed by a dynamic STORE with the same mask is a symmetric permutation -- a mask that selects the wrong registers writes the same bytes back out, and that mutant SURVIVED.  The case now clears FP0-FP2, loads through the dynamic mask, and writes out through a STATIC list naming FP0-FP2, so a wrong mask leaves zeros where the images belong.

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| D1 | the mask read from `ext` instead of the register | the dynamic list itself | the wrong registers are loaded and the static store writes zeros |
| D2 | `an_ov` not applied for a dynamic list | the run-time step | A5 moves by one register instead of three |
| D3 | eight bytes per register instead of twelve | the step's size | A5 lands 12 bytes short |
| D4 | a dynamic list decodes a step of ZERO again | the fix itself, and the original bug | A0 ends at `$56FC` instead of `$56F4` -- stepped by four, the SIZE rule, not by twelve |
| D5 | the predecrement entry base without its `+ 12` | the other half of the fix | the register image lands twelve bytes below where A0 points |

**Where `t_fpu.s` stands now: it runs from section 1 to section 18's single-step test with NO sections skipped.**  Section 18 needs the trace bit, which is M9's remainder and belongs in M9's accounting, not M10's.  Everything M10's own list contained is in.

**M10.8 -- FBcc, FScc, FDBcc, FTRAPcc and BSUN, BUILT 2026-09-23.**  Seven mutants, all caught.  `t_fpu.s` said this blocks more of the file than everything else put together: sections 10, 11, 44 and the 3,158-line section 47, which is the full 16 x 32 predicate table.

**How it is built.**  The condition comes from the unit's FPSR condition codes, so all four forms decode to `CL_FPU` like the rest -- reading `fpcc` while a released operation is still running is the DATA hazard M10.1(c) named, and the serialise plus the `fp_bg` wait is what makes it safe.  FBcc takes its predicate from the OPCODE and the others from the extension word; bit 5 of the six-bit field ALIASES and is not a trap (WinUAE's `fpp_cond` masks with $1F; section 44 pins it), so only bits 4:0 are read.  A signalling predicate that meets an unordered result records BSUN in FPSR whether or not the trap is enabled, and takes vector 48 (format $0, own PC) when FPCR enables it.  FTRAPcc takes TRAPcc's frame: vector 7, format $2, the next PC and the faulting instruction's address.

**Three bugs, and the first belongs in everyone's notes.**
1. **A function called from a continuous assignment is not reliably re-evaluated when a signal it reads from the ENCLOSING SCOPE changes** -- Icarus does not put such signals in the assignment's sensitivity list.  The symptom was an FBcc whose TARGET was right and whose `redirect` was stale: the first branch after an FCMP took it only if the condition had already been true.  Every other micro-op builder in this file takes its values as arguments, and now so does this one.  Same family as M10.1's trap (a named block with a local `logic` inside a case item of a function makes the whole decode X): **Icarus is quiet about both, and the tell is a value that is right in a `$display` and stale in the logic**.
2. **`CL_NOP` has no writeback arm in EX**, so a micro-op that must WRITE a register cannot use it.  FBcc can (it only redirects); FDBcc cannot, and silently wrote nothing until it kept its own class.
3. **`fp_stt` is shared** with the operand, frame and FMOVEM paths, so a conditional that followed an FScc into memory inherited `FS_WR` and dispatched through the STORE path instead of its own.  **This is the third time a shared `P_FPU` register has caught a new class out** -- `fp_bg` in M10.2, `fp_crd` and `fp_stt` here -- so the rule is now explicit: a class that reuses the beat states must set EVERY one of them at entry.

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| B1 | the OGT row of the predicate table | one predicate | two branches go the wrong way and FSGT writes zero |
| B2 | `fp_bsun_go` tied low | the enabled BSUN trap | no vector 48 is taken |
| B3 | `fp_bsun_req` tied low | the BSUN status bit | FPSR's BSUN bit stays clear with the trap disabled |
| B4 | `fp_dbn` does not decrement | the FDBcc count | the loop never ends |
| B5 | `fp_sccv` always zero | the FScc byte | FSGT writes 0 instead of $FF |
| B6 | the predicate always from `ext` | FBcc's predicate source | three branches go the wrong way |
| B7 | `fp_stt` left as it was at entry | bug 3 above | the FDBcc after an FScc-to-memory does nothing |

**M10.7 -- the `$4130` state frame, the An override it needed, and FMOVECR, BUILT 2026-09-23.**  Six mutants, all caught.  `t_fpu.s` chose the order: test 260 showed the frame blocks §15 onwards rather than only §17 and §50, and test 44 showed FMOVECR immediately behind it.

**The An override, which is the piece with life beyond this milestone.**  Every `(An)+` / `-(An)` step in this core is computed by EA-calc from a byte count the DECODER knew -- the operand's size (M10.1), four per control register (M10.2), twelve per floating-point register (M10.4) -- and that works because the length of all of those is a property of the encoding.  A state FRAME is the first thing whose length is not: it depends on what the unit is holding when the instruction runs, so decode cannot size it and EA-calc cannot step by it.  `an_ov()` in `ap040_ea_fetch.v` is the general way out and is documented at the function rather than in the FSAVE path: the micro-op already carries `u0_v/u0_r/u0_val` and `u1_v/u1_r/u1_val` for address-register writes, so EA-fetch overrides the VALUE and leaves the register selection where EA-calc put it.  **Anything else with a run-time-sized step -- the `$4160` BUSY frame, a future MOVEM variant -- uses this hook rather than inventing another.**

**The frame.**  Thirteen longwords laid out as `lib/AP68040`'s `fsave_unimp_word` does, and the restore side latches them in `S_FREST_U`'s order.  A `-(An)` FSAVE walks its base back by the remaining 48 bytes at entry and hands An the new value through the override; an `(An)+` FRESTORE steps by the whole 52.  `fsave_ack` is pulsed with the last longword, so the unit drops the state only once the whole frame is out.

**FMOVECR** (opclass 010, specifier 111) has no effective address at all and was therefore never decoded to `CL_FPU`.  It now is, because the unit does two things a faked decode-side frame could not: it answers `unimp` AND captures the state a following FSAVE must extract.

**The mutant that needed a better test, and why it is worth remembering.**  A permuted payload field -- writing `cmd1` where `cmd3` belongs -- SURVIVES a save/restore/save round trip, because the restore side reads the same index back into the same field and the second frame is byte-identical.  An identity test cannot see a symmetric permutation.  The `.exp` now checks all thirteen longwords ABSOLUTELY, and the values are predictable from the rule: `cmd1` at index 4 is the FSIN's own extension word ($000E), `cmd3` at index 1 is $0026, index 2 is the reserved longword, and `fpt` (7-9) and `et` (10-12) are the destination register's and the source operand's images, both 1.0 because that is what `FSIN.X FP0` was given.

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| F1 | the frame header is `$4100_0000` | the frame itself | the first FSAVE writes IDLE and the round trip mismatches twelve times |
| F2 | `an_ov` does nothing | the run-time-sized step | A0 ends 44 bytes short and the format error is never taken |
| F3 | `fsave_ack` never pulsed | "extracted, not copied" | a third FSAVE with no FRESTORE finds the state still there |
| F4 | one restore field dropped | the payload's way back in | the round trip mismatches |
| F5 | FMOVECR not decoded | the unit's `unimp` and its state capture | the handler's RTE pops the wrong frame size and the program walks away |
| F6 | `cmd1` written where `cmd3` belongs | the payload's layout | caught only by the ABSOLUTE check, not by the round trip |

**Where `t_fpu.s` stops now**: at section 18's single-step test, which needs the **trace bit** -- M9's remainder, not an FP gap at all.  Sections 10, 11 and 13's dynamic FMOVEM are still skipped for the FBcc family and the dynamic mask.

**M10.6 -- `t_fpu.s` run as the oracle, 2026-09-23, and what it says M10 still needs.**  The reference's own FPU program (5,241 lines, 50 sections) was run on the wrapper's compat leg, where `AP040_HAS_FPU` is 1 and the real unit is attached.  It stops at the first failing check, so the picture was taken with a new tool, `tb/mk_tfpu.py`, which makes three kinds of variant: `--diag` reports the stacked FORMAT/VECTOR word instead of the generic handler-failure number, `--diagpc` reports the low word of the stacked PC (which names the instruction), and `--skip N` drops a whole section so the run carries on into the next one.  Every skip is recorded below with what it hid, because the sections share cumulative counters and skipping far enough eventually invalidates the run -- which it did, and that is why the table stops being MEASURED where it does.

| § | what it exercises | result | which gap it needs |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1-9 | cache-hot entry, register reset, integer load/store, the FPSR condition codes, the single / double / extended formats, register-direct, `-(An)`/`(An)+` | **PASS** (measured) | -- |
| 10 | FCMP and **FBcc** | **was FAIL at the first `fbgt`; FIXED the same day (M10.8)** | -- |
| 11 | **FScc / FDBcc** | fixed with it (M10.8) | -- |
| 12 | FMOVEM of the registers, static list | **PASS** (measured) | -- (M10.4) |
| 13 | the control registers | **was PASS until its DYNAMIC FMOVEM mask; FIXED the same day (M10.9)** | -- |
| 14 | FSAVE / FRESTORE | **PASS** (measured) | -- (M10.5, the NULL and IDLE frames are all this section needs) |
| 15 | unimplemented instructions (vector 11) | **was FAIL at its first check; FIXED the same day and now runs to test 260** (measured) | **the FP effective-address legality rules, now built.**  A malformed FP EA -- an address register as a source, a data register too narrow for the format, an illegal FMOVE-out destination -- is the ORDINARY F-line, format $0 with the own PC, and that closes M10.0's recorded over-generosity.  **But only for an opmode the unit implements**: `t_fpu.s` test 326 (`fint.x a1,fp1`) pins the other half -- an FPSP-emulated opmode reports as UNIMPLEMENTED (vector 11, format $2, next PC) whatever its effective address is, because WinUAE runs `fault_if_unimplemented_680x0` before it rejects a Dn or An source.  Decode now carries a copy of the unit's `op_in_hw` list to tell the two apart (D22) |
| 15 cont. | ... and then the FSAVE of a pending unimplemented state | **was FAIL at test 260; FIXED the same day (M10.7)** | the `$4130` frame, built |
| 16-17 | the unimplemented frame's fields, the FSAVE/FRESTORE round trip | **PASS** (measured, once the frame was built) | -- |
| 18 | single-stepping an unimplemented FP instruction | **FAIL at test 218** (measured) | **the TRACE bit -- M9's remainder, not an FP gap.**  This is where `t_fpu.s` stops today |
| 41 | FMOVECR | **was FAIL at test 44; FIXED the same day (M10.7)** | it now reaches the unit, which answers `unimp` and captures the state |
| 16-18 | the unimplemented frame's fields, the FSAVE/FRESTORE round trip, single-stepping an unimplemented instruction | not measurable by skipping (the run desyncs once §15 is dropped: the counters and the handlers' state are cumulative) | the `$4130` frame (M10.5's gap) for 17; 18 also needs the trace bit, which is M9's remainder |
| 19-30 | arithmetic, boundary data types, unsupported data types, FSGLMUL/FSGLDIV, rounding precision, gradual underflow, denormals in every format, NaN signs, range control | predicted **PASS or near it** -- this is the unit's own datapath, which is lifted unchanged and was validated by cputest in the reference | -- expected; §21's vector 55 path is M10.1's and is tested by `fpustub.s` |
| 26, 42 | FP effective addresses a register cannot hold; the nonexisting opmode | predicted FAIL | the same format $0 EA-legality rule as §15 |
| 34-40, 43 | FDIV/FSQRT specials, FCMP corners, the FPCR's sixteen bits, opmode $78-$7F | predicted PASS | -- |
| 41 | **FMOVECR** | predicted FAIL | FMOVECR is not decoded to `CL_FPU` at all (it has no EA), so it takes format $4 instead of the unit's `unimp` |
| 44, 47 | the FBcc/FScc predicate aliases, and **the full 68040 predicate table, 16 x 32** | fail | the FBcc family again -- §47 alone is 3,158 lines of the file, so this is where the FBcc work pays |
| 45, 46 | FMOVEM register-mapping quirks; control-register move refinements | predicted PASS (M10.4 and M10.2 implement exactly these quirks) | -- |
| 48-50 | the FRESTORE state lifecycle, enabled STORE exceptions, FSAVE pending-exception frames | predicted FAIL; **the frames are BUILT since M10.10 (2026-09-24)** and upstream's `t_fpu_frames.s` passes, but `t_fpu.s` itself does not reach here yet (it stops at §47's memory-indirect FP EA, test 241) | a store's enabled exception (M10.3's gap) is still open |

**The order the table implies, cheapest real gap first** -- which is the order to work, not the order the sections appear:
1. ~~**The FP effective-address legality rules**~~ **DONE 2026-09-23 (M10.6)**: decode-only, and it took two rules rather than one -- the plain F-line for a hardware opmode, the unimplemented route for an FPSP one.  §15 now runs from its first check to test 260.
1b. **The `$4130` unimplemented-state frame**, which test 260 shows is the next thing in the way and is needed far earlier than predicted: FSAVE must EXTRACT the pending state the unit is holding (`fstate_*`, 13 longwords) instead of writing IDLE, and FRESTORE must install it again.  This is the piece whose `-(An)` step is variable, so it is where the explicit `u0_val` from EA-fetch finally has to be built.
2. **FBcc / FScc / FDBcc** (§10, §11, §38, §44 and the 3,158-line §47): the `fpcc` wire exists and is tied off; what is missing is the predicate evaluator, the decode, and BSUN (`bsun_req`/`bsun_enable`, also tied off).  This unblocks more of the file than everything else put together.
3. **The dynamic FMOVEM mask** (§13): a register read before the transfers, which `P_FPU` does not sequence yet.
4. **FMOVECR** (§41): route it to the unit so it answers `unimp`, which is what the FPSP expects.
5. **The `$4130`/`$4160` state frames** (§17, §48, §50) and **a store's enabled exception** (§49): the two recorded gaps of M10.5 and M10.3.

**M10.5 -- FSAVE and FRESTORE, the NULL and IDLE frames, BUILT 2026-09-23** (Q17 ruled: build it, and every image stays `AP040_HAS_FPU = 0`).  Six mutants, all caught.

**What it does.**  FSAVE writes ONE longword -- `$0000_0000` when the unit has never been used, `$4100_0000` once it has -- and FRESTORE reads one and dispatches on it: version byte 0 resets the unit, `$4100_0000` makes it idle-but-used, and **anything else takes the FORMAT ERROR, vector 14 with format $0 and the instruction's own PC**, which is the reference's own default arm.  That last choice is deliberate: the `$4130` and `$4160` payloads are legal 68040 frames this core does not sequence, and refusing one loudly beats accepting a state it cannot install.  Nothing in this core ever PRODUCES those frames, so the only way to meet one is software that built it by hand or an FPSP -- and there is no FPSP.

**The An problem did not arise, and that is why this subset was chosen.**  A NULL/IDLE frame is four bytes, so the `-(An)` / `(An)+` step is four and the ordinary decoded byte count carries it; the explicit `u0_val` the design called for is not needed YET.  It will be, for the 13- and 25-longword frames, and the design note below still stands for whoever builds them.

**Two things this step had to fix in what was already there.**
1. **FSAVE and FRESTORE carry NO floating-point extension word**, so every class wire that reads `ext` had to be qualified on `opcode[8:6]` first.  Until now `CL_FPU` was only ever produced for `opcode[8:6] == 000`, so `fp_cr` and `fp_mvm` could read `ext` freely; the moment FSAVE becomes `CL_FPU` those wires would alias on whatever the EA's extension word happens to be.  There is now an `fp_gen` term and every one of them is behind it.
2. **FRESTORE's read fell into the OPERAND path.**  It uses `FS_RD` like an operand fetch, and the operand path's capture block was gated on `!fp_cr && !fp_mvm` but not on the new classes -- so when the frame landed it was written into `fp_buf` and, because `fp_beats` was 1, a COMMAND was pulsed at the unit.  The visible symptom was `fpu_used` coming back to 1 after a FRESTORE of NULL, i.e. the reset appearing not to work; the cause was three instructions away.  The tell, worth remembering: a class that reuses the beat states must be excluded from EVERY arm of the path it reuses, not just the ones it obviously touches.

**Mutants for M10.5** (`fpr:fpureal_sv`):

| # | mutation | what it removes | how it shows |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1 | `fp_svw` always `$4100_0000` | the NULL/IDLE choice | the first FSAVE writes IDLE, so Kickstart's probe would report an FPU that was never used |
| S2 | the `fp_rstp` pulse | FRESTORE of a NULL frame resetting the unit | FSAVE writes IDLE afterwards, and the program runs off |
| S3 | the `fp_idlp` pulse | FRESTORE of an IDLE frame | the following FSAVE never writes |
| S4 | the format error replaced by "accept it as IDLE" | the refusal | no trap is taken and A0 walks on |
| S5 | the `(An)+` / `-(An)` step forced to 8 | the four-byte frame size | the stack forms desynchronise (note: forcing it to 0 is a NO-OP, because `eacomp` then falls back to the size rule, which is also four -- recorded so the next person does not repeat that mutation) |
| S6 | the format error stacks format $2 | the frame rule | the format/vector delta reads $2000 |

**Recorded gaps of M10.5**, each a rule and not a hang: the `$4130` and `$4160` frames are not sequenced in either direction (FSAVE never extracts one, so a pending unimplemented state is LOST rather than saved, and FRESTORE refuses one); `fsave_ack` and `frestore_unimp` stay tied off for the same reason; and on the format error the `(An)+` update does not commit, because it rides a dispatch that never happens -- the manual does not say what a 68040 does there and the frame size is variable anyway, so this is written down rather than claimed.

**FSAVE / FRESTORE: the design as it was written before it was built** (the frames, and the An problem the bigger ones still have):

The frames, from `ap040_core.v` S_FSAVE1 (`:3595`) and S_FREST2 (`:3671`):
- **no state**: ONE longword -- `$0000_0000` when the unit has never been used (NULL) or `$4100_0000` when it has (IDLE).  This is the case Kickstart's probe and an ordinary context switch hit, and it is the whole of what the LC040 path needs.
- **an unimplemented-instruction or prepared e1 state**: thirteen longwords, header `$4130_0000` and a payload the unit supplies through its `fstate_*` outputs.  For `-(An)` the reference walks the base back by 48 ITSELF (`t_a <= ea_addr - 48`, and it rewrites An).
- **a deferred e3 arithmetic state**: twenty-five longwords, header `$4160_0000`, base `ea_addr - 96`.
- **FRESTORE** reads the first longword and dispatches on it: version byte 0 -> `fp_reset`; `$4100_0000` -> `frestore_idle`; `$4130_0000` -> read twelve more and `frestore_unimp`; `$4160_0000` -> twenty-four more; anything else -> **format error, vector 14**.  A completed FRESTORE replaces the state wholesale and discards a pending deferred exception with it.

**The problem, and it is structural.**  The frame's SIZE is not known at decode: it depends on what state the unit is holding.  Every `-(An)` step in this core is computed by EA-calc from a decoded byte count (`d.imm[6:0]`), which is exactly why M10.1(b), M10.2 and M10.4 could reuse one mechanism.  FSAVE breaks that: a `-(An)` FSAVE must subtract 4, 52 or 100 depending on a condition EA-fetch learns only when it looks at the unit.  Two ways out, and the choice should be made deliberately:
1. **Let EA-fetch write An with an explicit value.**  The micro-op already carries `u0_v/u0_r/u0_val` for address-register updates; if EA-fetch may override `u0_val`, FSAVE computes the base itself the way the reference does.  This is the smaller change and it keeps the restart model (the update rides the trailing micro-op, so a fault mid-frame leaves An alone).
2. **Decode the worst case and correct it.**  Ugly, and it would write An twice.

**And a system-level caution that belongs with the decision, not after it.**  With `HAS_FPU = 1` and FSAVE still F-lining, Kickstart's `fsave -(a7)` probe reports NO FPU and AmigaOS never emits a floating-point instruction -- which is safe.  The moment FSAVE answers with a NULL/IDLE frame the probe reports an FPU, AmigaOS starts using one, and the first transcendental (`FSIN`, `FETOX`, ...) takes `unimp` -> vector 11 -> the FPSP, which is NOT installed until 68040.library is.  So **making FSAVE work is what turns the remaining FPU gaps from dormant into live**, and it should land together with a decision about the FPSP (68040.library installs it; a bare Kickstart does not).  That is a question for Paul, recorded in section 9 as Q17.

**Still out of M10**: FSAVE/FRESTORE (the state frames, which is what the Kickstart probe and any context switch want), the dynamic register list (its mask comes from a data register, which `P_FPU` does not sequence -- it keeps M10.0's format $4 frame, the same recorded gap as a memory-indirect EA), a memory-indirect effective address, and a STORE's enabled arithmetic exception (M10.3's recorded gap).  After FSAVE/FRESTORE, `t_fpu.s` becomes the oracle.

**Step (d), the arithmetic exceptions: designed 2026-09-23 so it is not started cold.**  It is now REQUIRED, not optional: M10.2 made FPCR writable, so a program can enable an exception, and the unit's enabled-exception arm (`ap040_fpu.v:1887`) sets `exc_req` WITHOUT pulsing `done` -- EA-fetch would wait for a `done` that never comes.  The reference's shape, read out of `ap040_core.v`:

1. **A non-released operation** (`fpu_exc_req && !fp_st`, `:4217`): take the exception at once, `exc(vec, format $0, pc, pc_i)` -- **format $0 and the NEXT instruction's PC**, a post-instruction exception.  Note what the reference does NOT do there: no `(An)+` / `-(An)` update (the other arms of that state do it explicitly, this one does not), which differs from `unimp`/`unsupp` where the update stands.  In our pipeline that falls out for free -- taking the exception without dispatching drops the update micro-op -- so following the reference costs nothing, and the difference is worth a divergence row when it is tested.
2. **A RELEASED operation** (`fpu_bg && fpu_exc_req`, `:1944`): it cannot be delivered against an instruction that has left, so it becomes PENDING (`fpu_pend_exc`, `fpu_pend_vec`) and `fpu_bg` is cleared -- and clearing it is the part that must not be forgotten, because otherwise the next FP instruction waits at `P_START` for ever, which is exactly the hang M10.2's `fp_cr` bug showed.  The reference also pulses `pend_capture` so the unit prepares the FSAVE e1 frame; that port stays tied off until FSAVE exists, and the tie is already recorded in `ap040_fpu_tie.vh`.
3. **Delivery of the pending one** (`:3600`): in front of the NEXT floating-point instruction, `exc(pend_vec, format $0, pc_i, pc_i)` -- **the dispatching instruction's own PC**, a pre-instruction exception, FPSP style; FPIAR still names the one that faulted.  In the pipelined core this does NOT need a new term in `stepf`'s chain (the `m9s` lesson: ~0.9 ns): let the instruction enter `P_FPU` as usual and raise it there from `fp_st()`, before the request is pulsed -- `fp_st` already builds exceptions and `P_FPU` is off the dispatch chain.
4. **The store cases** (`:4239`-`:4300`) are the intricate part and can come last: an enabled integer-store SNAN/OPERR does not write the destination and takes **format $3** with the operand's EA (0 for a Dn destination); a float-format Dn store DOES write the register first; a memory store writes memory first and delivers after the last beat.

Test shape (`fpureal_exc.s`, drafted): DZ enabled and `1.0 / 0.0` for case 1 (vector 50, format $0, next PC); INEX2 enabled on a divide that is released for case 2/3 (the trap lands on the following FP instruction with ITS PC); then the enables cleared and the same operations running clean.  `vectors.inc` needs vectors 48-54 routed to one `v_fparith` symbol, defaulting to `unexp` as the others do.  Mutants: `fp_bg` not cleared on `exc_req` (the hang), the pending vector delivered with the faulting instruction's PC instead of the dispatching one, format $2 instead of $0, and the exception taken while `accepted` has not yet risen.

**What M10 still needs, in the order it can be built**: (d) the arithmetic exceptions -- now reachable, because FPCR's enable byte can be written, and REQUIRED before anything sets it, since the unit's enabled-exception arm does not pulse `done` and EA-fetch would wait for ever; then opclass 110/111 (FMOVEM of the FP registers, which needs the `fm_*` raw port); then FSAVE/FRESTORE, which is what `t_fpu.s` and the Kickstart probe want; then the memory-indirect EA and the remaining legality rules if evidence appears.

**M10.1 step 3, the design as it was written before it was built** (kept because it is the record of what was decided and why):

1. **Instantiate the unit.**  It goes where the MMU goes -- `rtl/compat/ap040_pipe_tg68k_compat.v`, inside `generate if (AP040_HAS_FPU)` -- and the bench gets a second define beside `FPU_STUB` so the same programs can run against either.  The command port is already wired; `ap040_fpu`'s remaining inputs tie off for this step: `cr_we = 0`, `bsun_req = 0`, `fm_we = 0`, `fsave_ack = 0`, every `frestore_*` = 0, `fp_reset = 0`, `pend_capture = 0`.  Its `exc_req`/`exc_vec` (the arithmetic traps, vectors 48-54) stay unconnected until (d), and that is a deliberate, recorded gap: an ENABLED arithmetic exception will be silently dropped until then, so the first programs must leave the FPCR enable byte at zero.
2. **`ia_we` / `ia_wdata` should be wired at the same time**, not later: the reference sets FPIAR on every dispatch that engages the unit (`fpu_iawe`), and it is one more field on the request -- `ia_we = fp_req`, `ia_wdata = i.pc`.
